According to the Computer Emergency Response Team of India (CERT-IN), the nation experienced 1.39 million cybersecurity incidents in 2022. This surge in cyberattacks and crimes against businesses and organizations has led to an increased demand for cyber insurance. Currently valued at $50–60 million, the cyber insurance market is anticipated to witness remarkable growth, with a projected compound annual growth rate (CAGR) of 27%–30% over the next three to five years. Globally, the cyber insurance market surpassed $12 billion in 2022 and is forecasted to exceed $63 billion by 2029, with a compound annual growth rate of over 26%.

## 15.6 Intersection of Law with AI in Actuary Science

The convergence of law and AI in actuary science is strongly advised in the context of deepfake and cyberattacks to improve rights protection, enforce regulations, and ensure that rules and regulations are properly implemented to support the insurance industry’s smooth operation. Actuaries are experts in evaluating and controlling financial risks, and they are now using AI technology to improve their analytical and decision-making skills. But the use of AI in actuarial science brings up important ethical and legal issues that need to be carefully considered.

## 15.7 Examining Legal Frameworks, Regulations, and Ethical Guidelines that Govern the Implementation of AI in Actuarial Processes

In India, the demesne of actuarial sciences operates within a regulatory framework aimed at ensuring transparency, fairness, and the protection of stakeholders’ interests. Currently, there is a notable absence of specific legislation governing AI in India, as highlighted by IT Minister Ashwini Vaishnav, owing to the myriad moral and ethical concerns surrounding AI development. Nonetheless, the primary legislation governing digital and electronic commerce governance in India remains The Information Technology Act, 2000 (IT Act). It is to be noted that this act directly does not address AI, but various actions are covered under various sections of this act. Section 43 of this act provides compensation where there is negligence in handling subtle personal data, which leads to breaches of data privacy when AI is involved in managing the data of users. Section 73A of the act deals with electronic governance of cybersecurity, data protection, and digital signatures, creating a legal structure for using electronic signatures and electronic records in AI-controlled actuarial processes.

In the benchmark case of Justice K.S. Puttaswamy (Retd.) v. Union of India (2017), the Apex Court of India confirmed that right to privacy is the fundamental right under Constitution of India, which underscores the guarantee of preserving personal data from AI systems. Recently, substantial Personal Data Protection Act, 2023 is enacted to establish all-inclusive legal framework for the protection of personal data demarcating the responsibilities and commitments for institutions handling the data. This act integrates the principles of accountability, persistence, consistence, restraints, and data localization and also dictates for the establishment of jurisdictional authority called as Data Protection Authority to overlook and supervise the proper enforcement of the provisions, regulations, and guidelines of the Act, which includes tailored decision-making and profiling, imposing the requirement of written consent in processing personal data consuming AI algorithms dealing with interests and rights of individuals.

With the infiltration of AI-generated contents, deliberations have textured regarding copyright proprietorship and liability in case of infringement under the Indian Copyright Act, 1957. This act provisioned for safeguarding exclusive rights of creator and prevents unauthorized use or reproduction without authority. In the case of Gramophone Company of India Ltd. V. Super Cassettes Industries Ltd. (2011), the High Court of Delhi pronounced that AI-generated music is devoid of human creativity and thus is barred for protection of copyright.

Beside these, National e-Governance Plan is articulated to empower society through providing online government services incorporating AI as a vital constituent to enhance accessibility and efficiency of services of e-governance. To this end, many government agencies have integrated AI processes to enhance public services and decision-making process.

Additionally, National Education Policy is introduced by Indian Government to include coding workshops for sixth-grade students to make India as hub of e-innovations. The AI Research Analytics and Knowledge Assimilation Platform (AIRAWAT) is formulated by Indian Planning Commission called as Niti Ayog mandating for AI-equipped processes.

These are helpful in the insurance sector and are governed by Insurance Act, 1938, which provide regulations for actuarial assessment, solvency margins, and reporting obligations that are vital for operation of insurance business.

Actuaries play a significant role in assuring amenability with the regulatory requirements. The regulatory authority to overlook the insurance industry in India is Insurance Regulatory and Development Authority of India as per IRDAI Act, 1999. Insurance Companies are directed to adhere to regulations and standards fixed by IRDAI, which includes financial reporting, product design, and underwriting with the responsibility of actuaries for their compliance and application.

According to IRDAI, the panel actuaries have to work in accordance with various regulations, viz., IRDAI (Appointed Actuary) Regulations, 2017; IRDAI (Investment) Regulations, 2016 and IRDAI (Insurance Services by Banks) Regulations, 2002, etc., for protecting the interest of policyholders. For valuations, the IRDAI (Appointed Actuary) Regulations, 2017 were enacted to set the wide-ranging canons for the appointment and obligations of actuaries in insurance firms, which are vital for valuation, reserve establishment and guaranteeing pecuniary solidity. IRDAI (Investment) Regulations, 2016 set the guidelines for the operational investments for actuaries. IRDAI (Insurance Services by Banks) Regulations, 2002, fixed the guidelines for banks to provide insurance services.

Moreover, Companies Act, 2013 deals with establishment, registration, management, and dissolution of corporation, which require actuarial services in financial reserve assessment, reporting, and liability assessment. The Income Tax Act, 1961 provisioned for regulating pension schemes and insurance products with tax allusions for actuarial services for providing valuations.

Indian actuaries are often adhering to international norms to indorse quality and integrity of their work, viz., the International Standards of Actuarial Practice (ISAP), which are issued by International Actuarial Association (IAA) to provide guidelines on various actuarial practices. To protect the interest of stakeholders, Indian actuaries stay abreast of professional regulatory, legal, and ethical standards by following international and domestic norms.

IRDAI also issued policy guidelines in 2023 aiming to ensure proper compliance with all pertinent laws, regulations, standard guidelines, and codes including civil and criminal laws to meet regulatory, statutory, and contractual obligations which emphasized on compliance with security wants and information security laws. Statutes and contractual commitments to operate ethically and in consonance with the law of land, with active monitoring of compliance and prompt remedial actions in the case of breaches of standards. A clear strategy for ensuring compliance with relevant legal, statutory, regulatory, and contractual obligations is to be established and regularly updated.

Protocols must be established to ensure compliance with intellectual property rights and the usage of proprietary software products, with statutory rules made mandatory and formal risk analysis conducted to select controls addressing regulatory requirements and legal responsibilities. The legal department is tasked with providing guidance on legal requirements and ensuring compliance, promptly implementing necessary controls, and conducting regular compliance assessments. Regulatory circulars issued by bodies such as the Insurance Regulatory and Development Authority of India (IRDAI) mandate changes to security requirements and operational procedures, necessitating integration into insurance company operations to comply with IRDAI standards. Compliance with the Information Technology (IT) Act of 2000, its amendments, and relevant Acts is crucial, particularly regarding email communications, internet banking, and communication networks. Processes must be designed to align with revisions to the IT Act, 2000, or the introduction of supporting cyber laws or privacy legislation, with a deep understanding of the requirements of the IT Act, 2000 being essential for securing the organizational data environment and preventing fraud. Compliance with relevant laws, statutes, recommendations, and amendments to the Aadhaar Act is also imperative.

In support of these laws, rules, and regulations, guidelines are implemented to facilitate enforcement. The Information Technology (The Indian Computer Emergency Response Team (CERT-In)) Rules, 2013 empower the Computer Emergency Response Team to gather, analyse, and share cybersecurity incident data and enact emergency response measures. Guidelines issued by CERT-In in 2022 complement existing cybersecurity incident reporting obligations under the 2013 rules. Industry-specific regulations issued by entities such as the Reserve Bank of India and the Securities Exchange Board of India require regulated entities to maintain cybersecurity standards; compliance with the Indian Penal Code 1860 and the Companies (Management and Administration) Rules 2014 is mandatory for businesses to secure electronic records and systems against unauthorized access and tampering.

### 15.7.1 Institutional Framework

Considering that cybersecurity is a multifaceted issue, India has a sophisticated institutional architecture that spans many ministries, departments, and agencies, all of which play important roles in the field. For example, policy concerning IT, electronics, and the internet, including cyber legislation, is handled by the Ministry for Electronics and Information Technology (MeitY). As a nodal body for managing and coordinating cyber incident response operations, it established the CERT-In.

### 15.7.2 Security Measures

Under the IT Act, organizations handling sensitive personal data are obligated to adhere to state security requirements. These are delineated in the SPDI regulations, which stipulate that companies must implement operational, technological, managerial, and physical security controls. Moreover, in alignment with the ISO/IEC 27001 global standards for information security management, these regulations necessitate that body corporates undergo audit assessments conducted by an impartial, government-approved auditor at least annually, or more frequently should they significantly expand their computing resources and procedures. Nodal authorities and sectoral regulators also enforce security measures.

### 15.7.3 Cyber Incident Reporting

In line with the regulations set forth in 2013, organizations are obligated to promptly notify CERT-In of cybersecurity incidents as they arise. These incidents encompass a range of potential threats including denial of service attacks, phishing and ransomware incidents, website defacement, and targeted network or website scanning.

## 15.8 Regulatory Challenges

Onerous regulatory constraints may cause a lengthy, steep, and capital-intensive burnout for these start-ups, and an unfavourable regulatory climate can be a major impediment to appropriate implementation and data access through InsurTech. According to Insurance Act, 1938 (Section 2C to 5), companies wishing to enter the insurance policy market through the sale, issuance, origination, or underwriting of insurance policies must first register and, upon registration, get the necessary licenses under the Insurance Act. These licenses must be renewed yearly. After each calendar year ends, create a balance sheet, a profit and loss account, and a revenue account in the required forms for each kind of insurance business according to Insurance Act, 1938 (Sections 10, 11, 12).

## 15.9 Ambiguities in Legal System of AI in India

The Indian landscape for AI encounters several hurdles. Currently, India lacks specific legislation tailored explicitly to address AI. While existing laws such as the Personal Data Protection Act of 2023 and the Information Technology Act of 2000 touch upon aspects related to AI, they fall short of comprehensively tackling the intricate complexities and challenges posed by AI technologies. A significant barrier obstructing AI research and adoption in India lies in the absence of clear and enforceable ethical guidelines. This deficiency in robust standards may result in inconsistent behaviours and potential misuse of AI systems.

## 15.10 Cyber Insurance in Indian Banking Sector

In accordance with provision of I4C, the “National Cyber Crime Reporting Portal” has been developed to allow the public to report occurrences related to all forms of cybercrimes, with a concentration on cybercrimes against women and children. The law enforcement agencies in question in each state or territory handle cybercrime occurrences that are reported on this platform, turn them into formal complaints, and take appropriate action in accordance with the law. In order to prevent financial criminals from embezzling money, the “Citizen Financial Cyber Fraud Reporting and Management System,” under I4C, has been introduced. In response to more than 4.7 lakh complaints, the Citizen Financial Cyber Fraud Reporting and Management System has saved over Rs. 1,200 crore with the help of a free Helpline with the number “1930.”

## 15.11 Importance of Cyber Insurance in Indian Banking Sector

In today’s advancing technological landscape, cybersecurity holds immense significance, particularly in the banking sector where maintaining customer trust and credibility forms the cornerstone. With the increasing shift towards cashless transactions and the widespread use of virtual currencies such as debit and credit cards, ensuring robust cybersecurity measures becomes imperative to safeguard stakeholder data and uphold privacy standards. Instances of data breaches not only undermine the trust of customers in banks but also pose a critical challenge for banking institutions.

According to DSCI India cyber threat report (2023), Data Security Council of India (DSCI) in collaboration with SEQRITE released the data on cybercrimes as given in [Table 15.4](#ch15.xhtml#t15_4).

In the face of an expanding threat landscape driven by the rapid increase in digitization, effective management of cyber risks becomes paramount. In this context, cyber insurance emerges as a pivotal strategy for both managing and mitigating risks, providing an additional incentive for the adoption of preventive measures encompassing products, services, and best practices. Tailored cyber insurance products serve to alleviate exposure to risk by covering expenses incurred in the aftermath of a cyberattack or breach.

## 15.12 Benefits from Cyber Insurance

The primary benefits of cyber insurance lie in its ability to provide coverage to organizations or individuals in the event of a cyberattack, mitigating financial losses and facilitating recovery. For businesses, cyber insurance can cover loss of revenue or additional expenses incurred during cyber incidents, offering protection against ransomware attacks to which hackers demand payment for data retrieval. Moreover, in cases of lost or corrupted digital assets, cyber insurance policies can assist with recovery and restoration costs. Additionally, in the event of a data breach, financial institutions or corporations are required to notify affected customers and address privacy infringement claims, with cyber insurance covering associated legal fees.

### 15.12.1 Data on Cyber Breach Cover

In a recent study carried out by cybersecurity firm Sophos (2023), findings unveiled that India ranks sixth among 14 nations concerning the proportion of businesses equipped with sufficient coverage for cyber breaches. This report includes 3,000 respondents for survey from the countries, which includes 300 respondents from India. The report probed into complicated dynamics among cybersecurity, cyber insurance, and ransomware. This report divulged that South Africa is at top with 98% organizations holding cyber insurance benefits, followed by Singapore with 97%, and Australia and the United States with 94%.

### 15.12.2 Data Recovery Cases

Foundations that are well-found with cyber insurance determines the suppleness in convalescing data post-ransomware occurrences when compared to those foundations that have not taken any protective measures of cyber insurance. Only 84% of foundations deficient in such insurance were successful in recovering their data.

The stringent cyber protocols mandated for eligibility in acquiring such policies strategically position organizations for effective data restoration, underscoring the imperative for secure backups and the adoption of a comprehensive incident response strategy. Additionally, the research indicates that victims of ransomware possessing standalone cyber insurance policies are nearly four times more inclined to opt for ransom payment to regain access to encrypted data than those lacking cyber coverage.

Across various industry verticals, the education sector, encompassing both higher and lower education, showcased the highest overall level of cyber insurance coverage at 96%. This was followed by the financial services sector, primarily favouring standalone cyber policies (59%), and the retail sector (56%). Notably, the IT, telecom, and technology sectors displayed a lower coverage rate of 35% ([Kurmanath 2023](#ch15.xhtml#r15_13)).

## 15.13 Legal Considerations Are Pivotal in Ensuring Ethical, Transparent, and Compliant Practices in Risk Assessment-Promoting Responsible AI in Insurance

Legal considerations are indeed crucial in ensuring ethical, transparent, and compliant practices in risk assessment within the field of actuarial sciences in India. Adhering to applicable laws and regulations helps protect the interests of stakeholders, ensures fair practices, and maintains the integrity of the actuarial profession. With the adoption of AI in actuarial processes, questions regarding legal liability may arise in cases of errors, biases, or malfunctions in AI systems. Legal liability frameworks in India, including contract law, consumer protection laws, and tort liability principles, may apply to determine liability for damages resulting from AI-related errors or misconduct.

## 15.14 Discussion

As a result of hiking, the rate of cyberattacks and cybercrimes, the utmost contemplation is protecting the customer data and to ensure privacy with the propagation of AI-empowered technologies, which mandates the actuarial sciences to update with technological advancement combating the striking rate of cybercrimes, hence contributing the insurance sector in minimizing the risk assessment. For this drive, there is need of collaborations of actuaries with data scientists, technology professionals, and legal connoisseurs to robust risk assessment frameworks. Such kind of interdisciplinary collaboration may foster novelty, augment risk assessment and management proficiencies, and warrant all-inclusive slants to address in curbing and monitoring legal challenges.

For better risk assessment for cybersecurity, actuaries have to observe data protection laws, viz., Personal Data Protection Act, 2023, to protect subtle information, hence preserving the trust of consumers. For this purpose, actuaries should also have to give priority to ethical contemplations in the development and dispositioning of AI algorithms. This includes considerations like biasness in data and algorithms, sponsoring non-discrimination and fair-mindedness and encouraging transparency in supervisory policymaking processes.

### 15.14.1 Conclusions

Conclusively, the juncture of AI-driven actuaries with the intersection of law provides a transformative and revolutionary landscape for minimizing the impending insurance risk assessment. The advanced technology experts, insurance, and actuaries’ experts along with legal doyens have to circumnavigate a regulatory milieu to guarantee fair, legal, ethical, and yielding practices to reduce cyber risks. For this purpose, regulations and strategies are laid down by regulatory bodies, viz., Insurance Regulatory and Development Authority of India (IRDAI); hence, there is a need for proper enforcement and compliance by the Risk Management Authorities in assessing risk management policies in insurance practices.

# 16 Charting the FutureArtificial Intelligence’s Role in Revolutionizing Actuarial Work in the Insurance Sector

Mufaro Dzingirai and Rukudzo Dzapasi

DOI: [10.1201/9781003472551-20](https://doi.org/10.1201/9781003472551-20)

## 16.1 Introduction

In a digitalized and globalized economy, the potential benefits of the adoption of artificial intelligence technologies across sectors are witnessed in modern societies and economies. Although sectors such as manufacturing, healthcare, software development, agriculture, and banking have been hailed for high uptake of artificial intelligence technologies and massive investment in artificial intelligence platforms, it is troublesome to observe that the insurance sector is largely neglected in the artificial intelligence movement and discourse.

In the insurance sector, it has been witnessed that artificial intelligence is revolutionizing the ways in which business is being conducted in the 5th Industrial Revolution. With the radical transformation of technology in the contemporary business world, insurance companies are adopting artificial intelligence in both developed and developing countries ([Gürsev 2023](#ch16.xhtml#r16_6); [Khalisa 2024](#ch16.xhtml#r16_9); [Mangla et al. 2022](#ch16.xhtml#r16_18); [Prince & Taylor 2023](#ch16.xhtml#r16_26)). Artificial intelligence is playing a significant role in transforming the global insurance sector by revolutionizing various aspects of the industry, such as customer experience, underwriting, claims processing, fraud detection, and risk assessment. Although insurance companies in developed countries are at an advanced stage when it comes to the implementation of artificial intelligence, insurance companies in developing countries are also trying to accelerate the adoption of artificial intelligence. It is widely accepted that many organizations in developing countries are slowly adopting digital transformation mainly because they have weak financial health.

It is imperative to mention that the sustainability of various insurance firms depends on the extent to which the organizations adopt advanced technology ([Malifete et al. 2022](#ch16.xhtml#r16_17); [Shamsuddin et al. 2023](#ch16.xhtml#r16_28)). As such, it is the duty of a proactive manager to provide resources with the aim of improving efficiency and effectiveness. Given that we are now in a global economy, it makes sense to harness the benefits of artificial intelligence so as to outsmart the competitors. Investment in artificial intelligence can lead to the generation of real-time data that can assist in risk management. By ensuring effective risk management, the organization can enjoy market leadership, which can go a long way in ensuring competitive advantage.

Going forward, actuarial work in the insurance sector is becoming more data-based, which justifies the need for generative artificial intelligence. Given the importance of artificial intelligence in actuarial work, proactive insurers are implementing artificial intelligence technologies like natural language processing, machine learning, deep learning, and robotics processing automation as well as video and audio analysis with the aim to deliver exceptional products and services. It is worth noting that digital transformation in the form of artificial intelligence has improved speed, accuracy, and efficiency across branches of insurance companies ([Gürsev 2023](#ch16.xhtml#r16_6); [Prince & Taylor 2023](#ch16.xhtml#r16_26); [Shamsuddin et al. 2023](#ch16.xhtml#r16_28)). Therefore, the need for relevant information technology infrastructure and mechanisms cannot be underestimated journeying toward data integration. Interestingly, artificial intelligence helps insurance companies to gather and analyze granular, timely, and accurate actuarial data.

It is a fact that artificial intelligence has been hailed for playing an instrumental role in accelerating digital transformation across sectors. With the need for actuarial excellence, a data culture strategy can support data-driven strategic decisions. Of late, it is slowly gaining momentum in the insurance sector ([Mangla et al. 2022](#ch16.xhtml#r16_18)). Notably, the adoption of artificial intelligence in the insurance sectors of developing countries is still in the early stages. Although generative artificial intelligence is being incorporated into the insurance sector, its potential is not yet fully realized ([Holland 2022](#ch16.xhtml#r16_7)). Consequently, this chapter aims to critically examine the role of artificial intelligence in revolutionizing actuarial work in the insurance sector.

## 16.2 Artificial Intelligence and Insurance Sector: A Global Perspective

The exponential increase of artificial intelligence adoption worldwide has been associated with several merits for the insurance sector. The Oxford English dictionary defines artificial intelligence as “the theory and development of computer systems able to perform tasks normally requiring human intelligence, such as visual perception, speech recognition, decision-making, and translation between languages”. With this in mind, global insurers allocate a significant portion of their limited financial resources toward the adoption of artificial intelligence technologies. For instance, a big insurer in Hong Kong invested HK$20 million in artificial intelligence technology in an attempt to shorten claims processing time ([KPMG 2023](#ch16.xhtml#r16_10)). Artificial Intelligence-powered chatbots and virtual assistants are being used to provide personalized customer service, answer queries, and assist with policy inquiries, resulting in enhanced customer experience. These virtual agents can handle multiple requests simultaneously, leading to faster response times and improved customer satisfaction. This supports the notion that generative artificial intelligence is now a topical issue in corporate boardrooms with the aim of augmenting corporate strategies.

There is no doubt that artificial intelligence improves actuarial work in terms of artificial intelligence-enabled marketing, optimization of technological capabilities and competencies, improving customer experiences by using chatbots, automation of routine tasks, fraud deterrence, claims modelling, and robust risk management models. In this regard, artificial intelligence algorithms play a significant role in analyzing voluminous data in supporting precise pricing models when it comes to the insurance sector ([KPMG 2023](#ch16.xhtml#r16_10)). Therefore, it would be very easy for insurers to offer customized products and services, assess customer profiles, and then reduce risks. More interestingly, by leveraging artificial intelligence, data analysts can extract meaningful and valuable insights from voluminous unstructured and structured data collected from clients. Accordingly, artificial intelligence-enabled marketing strategies can be effectively implemented by insurers in this digitalized business world. According to [Lior (2021](#ch16.xhtml#r16_15), p. 467), “the artificial intelligence (“AI”) industry is predicted to grow exponentially over the next decade, up to a 14.5% contribution share to Gross Domestic Product in North America by 2030”. This shows how significant is artificial intelligence when it comes to socio-economic transformation.

In terms of OECD context, the adoption of the Recommendation on Artificial Intelligence was adopted in May 2019 and the Ethics Guidelines for Trustworthy Artificial Intelligence was also adopted in April 2019 ([OECD 2020](#ch16.xhtml#r16_22)). The OECD’s Insurance and Private Pensions Committee participated in discussing technology related to the insurance industry. It emerged that artificial intelligence was regarded as a powerful tool for automating the claims process, whereby OCR technology assists insurers in extracting relevant and accurate data from documents which can reduce errors associated with manual data entry. In addition, artificial intelligence helps insurers when applying predictive data analytics when it comes to the assessment of risk and the development of risk-elimination strategies. Admittedly, artificial intelligence algorithms can be used to predict potential risks and assist data analysts in formulating preventive measures. Consequently, artificial intelligence is a game changer in the insurance sector. It requires a comprehensive understanding of how new artificial intelligence projects in the insurance sector can deliver long-term benefits to insurers and their clients ([Holland 2022](#ch16.xhtml#r16_7)).

## 16.3 Drivers of the Adoption of Artificial Intelligence in the Insurance Sector

With the widespread impact of advanced technologies in the corporate world, it is worth capturing the key drivers of the adoption of artificial intelligence in the insurance sector. Firstly, the availability of large data sources is considered to be one of the drivers of artificial intelligence adoption in actuarial work. This implies that large databases play an instrumental role in applying artificial intelligence in areas such as risk management and claims processing in the insurance sector ([Leech et al. 2024](#ch16.xhtml#r16_14)). Secondly, the exponential rise in machine learning is also another chief drive of artificial intelligence adoption in the insurance sector. It is in this context that machine learning deals with training algorithms to learn from bid data, which is widely regarded as a subset of artificial intelligence ([Baruah & Singh 2023](#ch16.xhtml#r16_2); [Poufinas et al. 2023](#ch16.xhtml#r16_25)). It is interesting to note that researchers can teach and train machines to make predictions and recognize patterns. Machine learning also encompasses the utilization of a plethora of applications that recognize speech and images. To this end, deep learning and neural networks can recognize problematic patterns in relation to sounds, text, and pictures.

Thirdly, perceived usefulness is another key driver of artificial intelligence adoption in the insurance industry. It is within this context that the insurers perceive artificial intelligence as a powerful tool for detecting and monitoring fraud. More interestingly, artificial intelligence is widely considered to be a strategic approach to risk reduction and mitigation in actuarial work. The application of a robust risk and network model helps to improve efficiency and effectiveness in the current digitalized world ([Bao et al. 2022](#ch16.xhtml#r16_1); [Gupta et al. 2021](#ch16.xhtml#r16_5); [Nabrawi & Alanazi 2023](#ch16.xhtml#r16_21); [Owolabi et al. 2023](#ch16.xhtml#r16_23)). Fourthly, massive investment in research and development has been hailed as one of the key drivers of artificial intelligence in actuarial work. It is interesting to note that both government and private players are investing in research that is related to the application of artificial intelligence to different sectors of the economy although developing countries are lagging behind in terms of supporting research and development when it comes to artificial intelligence (Leal-Filho et al. 2023; [Marino et al. 2023](#ch16.xhtml#r16_19)). This is the reason why most of the insurers are at the infancy stage of artificial intelligence adoption. The investment in research and development has led to the creation of robotics, computer vision, and natural language processing.

Fifthly, the demand for customized products and services necessitated the adoption of artificial intelligence in actuarial work. The prediction models supported by artificial intelligence allow insurers to deliver personalized insurance products and services ([Khalisa 2024](#ch16.xhtml#r16_9); [Singh et al. 2022](#ch16.xhtml#r16_29); [St-Onge et al. 2022](#ch16.xhtml#r16_31)). Artificial intelligence technologies allow actuarial scientists to learn from big data about customer profiles. Sixthly, the rise in cloud computing also acts as the fertile ground that supports artificial intelligence adoption in the insurance market. It is interesting to observe that the emergence of cloud computing permitted voluminous data to be easily processed and stored in a manner that is scalable and cost-effective. Consequently, insurers and actuarial scientists can easily deploy artificial intelligence systems. Seventhly, Society 5.0 is another key factor that influences the adoption of artificial intelligence in actuarial science. Society 5.0 is a new society dominated by technological innovation, whereby humans address wicked problems by offering valuable technological solutions. With this in mind, it is not surprising that Society 5.0 has been associated with a massive explosion of big data. As such, voluminous data has permitted the application of machine and deep learning so as to augment artificial intelligence systems.

Eighthly, the support of innovative culture has demanded insurers to embrace artificial intelligence. It has been observed that the support of an innovative culture in the organization can lead to sustainable growth of the company ([Malifete et al. 2022](#ch16.xhtml#r16_17); [Loh & Soo 2023](#ch16.xhtml#r16_16)). Therefore, insurance companies dominated by innovative culture can find it easy to embrace artificial intelligence. The sustainability of the company in a turbulent and dynamic business environment hinges on the adoption of advanced technology like artificial intelligence technologies and systems. Lastly, competitive pressure is another factor that can influence artificial intelligence adoption in the insurance sector. With this in mind, it is worth mentioning that the insurance market is associated with cut-throat competition ([Pathak & Bansal 2024](#ch16.xhtml#r16_24); [Tanwar & Bhardwai 2022](#ch16.xhtml#r16_32)). As such, managers of insurance are forced to adopt artificial intelligence so as to reduce costs, improve efficiency, and access real-time data that assists managers in making informed strategic decisions.

## 16.4 Challenges Faced in Fostering Artificial Intelligence in Actuarial Work in the Insurance Sector

Despite the fact that artificial intelligence is relevant to the insurance sector, there are many challenges that can be faced by insurers when adopting artificial intelligence. To enjoy the benefits of artificial intelligence, the managers of insurance companies must address the hindrances of artificial intelligence adoption. Limited availability of quality data for artificial intelligence models is one of the challenges that can be encountered when adopting artificial intelligence. While actuarial work has been hailed for dealing with issues of in-depth financial records analysis, policyholder profile analysis, and extrapolation of historical data, it is a great challenge to assemble accurate data in many developing countries owing to a lack of expertise. Consequently, there is a challenge in training models of artificial intelligence using unreliable data.

According to [Lauterbach (2019)](#ch16.xhtml#r16_12), insurance companies are mainly faced with a challenge related to difficulties in interpreting artificial intelligence algorithms mainly because of a lack of model transparency. With such difficulties, insurers are advocating for artificial intelligence training in order to have a broader and deeper understanding of artificial intelligence systems. Thus, the insurance sector needs to develop methods to make artificial intelligence algorithms more transparent and interpretable with the main purpose of ensuring trust and accountability.

In addition, ethical considerations and potential biases are also considered to be posing a significant challenge when it comes to artificial intelligence adoption. It is a fact that actuarial work deals with large data, which requires adherence to anti-discriminatory and respect for the privacy of the clients ([Chikhaoui et al. 2022](#ch16.xhtml#r16_4); [Prince & Taylor 2023](#ch16.xhtml#r16_26); [Wilkinson et al. 2024](#ch16.xhtml#r16_33)). Worryingly, the historical data that is required for modeling can also be biased. In this regard, if unreliable data is used to train artificial intelligence models, there will be a great challenge of perpetuating existing biases especially when the insurers are making strategic decisions. With this in mind, it is imperative to augment compliance with laws and ethical considerations, which can be challenges to insurers.

## 16.5 Methodology

This study adopted a comprehensive literature review methodology to establish the role of artificial intelligence in revolutionizing actuarial work within the insurance sector. It was deemed an appropriate methodology given that the extant literature related to artificial intelligence in actuarial work is scattered and fragmented. This research methodology allowed the researchers to gather data from secondary sources. These sources include Web of Science, Google Scholar, organizational sources, and the Scopus database. Collected data was analyzed according to emerged patterns which is in tandem with thematic analysis. The results and discussion are reported as follows.

## 16.6 Role of Artificial Intelligence in Actuarial Work in Insurance Sector

It emerged that artificial intelligence plays an instrumental role in supporting actuarial work in the current digitalized economy. With the successful implementation of artificial intelligence in the insurance sector, companies are more interested in boosting productivity. This can be achieved by effectively and efficiently synthesizing big data extracted from customer profiles using machine learning. Insurance companies that leverage artificial intelligence have the potential to automate tasks that are done repetitively including data processing, data entry and verification as well as underwriting process. In this context, [Gupta et al. (2021)](#ch16.xhtml#r16_5) argued that insurance companies that have a high affinity for artificial intelligence can outsmart their rivalry as they can streamline workflows and enhance decision-making processes in a manner that fosters advanced risk assessment.

Insurance companies that command market leadership are those that focus on personalized services. As such, artificial intelligence provides a benefit in enhancing customer experience. This can be done in the form of mixed reality and augmented reality. As a matter of fact, the application of artificial intelligence could allow insurance firms to engage customers personally by embracing robots and virtual assistants. It is a fact that personalized customer service is one of the positive business outcomes that can be derived from the efficient utilization of artificial intelligence. Customers can have various queries 24/7, which implies that it is imperative to employ artificial intelligence in dealing with such queries with the aid of online chatbots.

It is common knowledge that robust risk assessment is at the epicenter of business success in the insurance industry. Undoubtedly, advanced technologies like artificial intelligence can be powerful tools to deal with the negative effects of poor risk assessment in the insurance sector. In the same vein, fraud is one of the forms of risk that can be encountered by insurers, which demands the utilization of machine learning and neural networks. These kinds of technology can assist in real-time risk assessment, prediction of fraudulent claim activities, and accurate risk computation ([Brown 2018](#ch16.xhtml#r16_3)). It is imperative to observe that artificial intelligence provides many benefits when insurance companies are dealing with claims management. In claims management, artificial intelligence plays a catalyst role in providing closer cross-examination of suspicious accounts. This suggests that red-flagging is very crucial in claims management, which demands the use of neural networks and deep learning.

In light of the above, cost-containment approaches embedded in artificial intelligence can add value to insurance especially when managing customer claims ([Kewal & Saxena 2023](#ch16.xhtml#r16_8); [Khalisa 2024](#ch16.xhtml#r16_9); [Sinha et al. 2021](#ch16.xhtml#r16_30)). The claims related to on-demand insurance, usage-based insurance, and micro-insurance can be easily automated with the application of artificial intelligence. The main motive for automating claims management processes is to collect real-time data from various data sources like sensors and biometric machines utilizing trained artificial intelligence models. This can go a long way in assisting insurers to expedite their claims processes and introduce unique and innovative products and services. With the utilization of artificial intelligence models, insurance firms can unlock the potential values of real-time data and deliver excellent insurance products and services efficiently.

Artificial intelligence can quicken the process of new product development. It is evident that new insurance products and services are supported by the digitalization of the insurance process. Within the context of micro-insurance, on-demand insurance, and usage-based insurance, the need for portfolio innovation is at the center of excellent service delivery in the insurance sector ([Khalisa 2024](#ch16.xhtml#r16_9); [Kumari et al. 2024](#ch16.xhtml#r16_11); [McDonald et al. 2024](#ch16.xhtml#r16_20)). Therefore, it is not surprising to mention that artificial intelligence helps insurance companies to accelerate their internal processes that support the development of new product portfolios. The artificial intelligence models can derive valuable insights that can be effectively utilized in the new product or service development process, and such an approach helps in streamlining the business operations.

Artificial intelligence can also help in developing robust risk prediction models in insurance work. This implies that advanced technologies can assist actuarial scientists in computing risk levels associated with different products and services. To be specific, machine learning plays a fundamental role in determining policy pricing systems in actuarial work ([Khalisa 2024](#ch16.xhtml#r16_9); Sadowski 2023; [Xin & Huang 2023](#ch16.xhtml#r16_34)). This suggests that price optimization demands the application of artificial intelligence technologies. The refinement of pricing models in the insurance sector can be promoted by the use of data from telematics. These kinds of models can be applied to synthesize large amounts of data taking into account different variables and can be built upon to create user-friendly tools for non-technical staff, such as underwriters. More interestingly, predictive and prescriptive analytics models can be useful in gaining an in-depth understanding of improving operational resilience, risk computation, predictive customer behavior, and enhancing customer interaction in the insurance sector. To this end, the power of artificial intelligence within the context of underwriting and pricing perspectives cannot be underestimated.

More interestingly, artificial intelligence adoption can offer real-time and accurate data, which is of great importance to strategic managers of insurance firms. It is widely accepted that evidence-based management in the insurance sector demands the instant provision of structured data. Therefore, prudent managers of insurance companies are embracing artificial intelligence at an alarming rate, especially in developed countries. The availability of policy data, market trends, and claims history forces managers to look for advanced models of analyzing big data in the insurance market. Interestingly, artificial intelligence tools including machine learning algorithms allow insurers to extract and draw valuable insights from collected structured and unstructured data. This not only enables them to make informed decisions based on data but also helps in improving their operational processes, which is undoubtedly a game-changer in the industry.

Going forward, artificial intelligence adoption provides a benefit in terms of improving the underwriting process in the insurance sector. It is very difficult to keep up with advanced technology without embracing advanced artificial intelligence technologies that can assist in the underwriting processes ([Khalisa 2024](#ch16.xhtml#r16_9); [Wilkinson et al. 2024](#ch16.xhtml#r16_33)). Application of artificial intelligence models has transformed the insurance’s process of underwriting by utilizing predictive analytics. The quotes on demand and customization of policies can be done with the aid of artificial intelligence. Manual underwriting is no longer admirable when it comes to property, life, and casual insurance. Artificial intelligence technologies make the insurance underwriting process so fast and cost-effective. It is necessary at this juncture to note that artificial intelligence plays an instrumental role in ensuring robust monitoring and evaluation. With the adoption of artificial intelligence, it is much easier to monitor and evaluate the progress of the actuarial work. Artificial intelligence helps insurers to secure real-time structured and unstructured data associated with indicators of progress in terms of claims management and underwriting.

## 16.7 Recommendations

Given the instrumental role of artificial intelligence in promoting efficiency in the insurance sector, the following are the proposed recommendations:

*   **Development of regulatory framework:** It is recommended that the regulators of different economies can come up with regulatory frameworks related to artificial intelligence in the insurance sector. This could ensure that the rights and privacy of clients are respected when applying artificial intelligence models.
*   **Tax relief on artificial intelligence technologies:** Governments are recommended to apply tax relief on the importation of artificial intelligence technologies. This can assist insurers in reducing the procurement costs of artificial intelligence tools and technologies. This is more applicable to most of the developing countries that rely on the importation of advanced technologies.
*   **Artificial intelligence knowledge hubs:** The institutions of higher learning especially in African countries should establish artificial intelligence hubs. These hubs can be the center for training and development when it comes to the development and application of artificial intelligence technologies and tools.
*   **Public–private partnerships:** In this digitalized economy, it is recommended that the public and private players should work together in intensifying artificial intelligence adoption in insurance. This could assist in the sharing of knowledge and expertise.
*   **Grants from non-governmental organizations:** Non-governmental organizations are hereby recommended to offer grants to insurance companies associated with limited financial resources. These grants can be channeled toward harnessing the benefits of artificial intelligence.
*   **Artificial intelligence awareness campaigns:** It is recommended that the regulators in the insurance sector should design and implement a plethora of artificial intelligence awareness campaigns so as to sensitize the need for artificial technology in the current digital corporate world.

## 16.8 Conclusion

This chapter has critically examined the role of artificial intelligence in revolutionizing actuarial work in the insurance sector. Despite the fact that some sectors of the economy such as manufacturing, healthcare, software development, agriculture, and banking have been hailed for high uptake of artificial intelligence technologies and massive investment in artificial intelligence platforms, the insurance sector is largely neglected in this artificial intelligence movement and discourse. In the current 5th industrial revolution, the actuarial work can be made easier through the adoption of advanced digital technologies that support the transformation of the insurance sector especially with the aid of artificial intelligence technologies. As such, it was observed that artificial intelligence technologies are of utmost importance when it comes to the insurance sector in the sense that they boost productivity, augment customer experience through personalized services, foster automation of claims management, reduce fraud, support the development of customer predictive models, offer real-time and accurate data, improve underwriting process, quicken new product development, and ensure robust monitoring and evaluation. The study concludes that artificial intelligence is a powerful strategy toward superior competitive advantage in the insurance sector. Therefore, it is recommended that the management of insurance companies should effectively and efficiently embrace artificial intelligence as a game-changer in transforming the performance of insurance companies.
